The [United States](3434750.md) has a [highly developed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) and diversified [market-oriented economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy).[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] It is the world's [largest economy by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)), generating 26% of global economic output.[^cite_note-IMF_datamapper-41][^cite_note-42] It is the [second-largest by purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)) (PPP). On a per capita basis, the U.S. ranks [ninth-highest nominal GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)_per_capita) and [10th-highest GDP per capita by PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita).[^cite_note-IMFWEOUS-6][^cite_note-43] The [U.S. dollar](18717338.md) is the world's foremost [reserve currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_currency) with [widespread international usage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_use_of_the_U.S._dollar) and serve as reference standard for the [petrodollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrodollar) and [eurodollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurodollar).[^cite_note-federalreserve.gov-44] Since the end of [World War II](32927.md), the U.S. economy has achieved relatively steady growth, [low unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment_in_the_United_States) and inflation, along with rapid advances in [technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_the_United_States). The American economy is fueled by high [productivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Productivity), [technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_the_United_States), well-developed [transportation infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_infrastructure), and extensive [natural resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_resource).[^cite_note-Wright,_Gavin_2007_p._185-45]

The nation is the [second-largest manufacturer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_manufacturing_output), with [American manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing_in_the_United_States) making up a fifth of the global total.[^cite_note-Vargo,_Frank-46] The [largest U.S. trading partners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_trading_partners_of_the_United_States) are [Mexico](3966054.md), [Canada](5042916.md), [China](5405.md), [Japan](15573.md), and [Germany](11867.md).[^cite_note-U.S._Census_Bureau-2016-47] The U.S. is the world's [largest importer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_imports) and [second-largest exporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_exports).[^cite_note-48] It has [free trade agreements](2533562.md) with [many countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade_agreements_of_the_United_States).[^cite_note-49] The country is the world's largest producer of [petroleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_oil_production), [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_production), and [blood products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_product).[^cite_note-lop-50][^cite_note-51] The U.S. has the world's largest [consumer market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_consumer_markets).[^cite_note-unstats.un.org-52] Within the American banking system, as of 2026, [commercial banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_bank) maintain \$25 trillion in assets.[^cite_note-53] The nation maintains a large [U.S. treasuries market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Treasury_security) and hosts the world's [largest gold reserves](1056205.md#Officially_reported_holdings).[^cite_note-54] It is home to 139 of the [500 largest companies globally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_S\&P_500_companies).[^cite_note-55]

Americans have the sixth-highest average [household](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Household_income) and [employee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_average_wage) income among [OECD](33853117.md) member states.[^cite_note-56] In 2026, they had the highest [median household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_household_income) among OECD countries,[^cite_note-57] with one of the world's highest [income inequalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality_in_the_United_States) among [developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_countries).[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] The U.S. has the world's [highest number of billionaires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_number_of_billionaires), with total wealth of \$5.7 trillion.[^cite_note-Forbes-60] The U.S. has a highly flexible [labor market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_labor_law) and [job security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Job_security) is relatively low.[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62] The nation's labor market has attracted [immigrants from all over the world](15051.md) and its [net migration rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_net_migration_rate) is among the highest in the world.[^cite_note-The_World_Factbook-63] The U.S. has a highly efficient [social security system](21109533.md); social expenditure stood at [roughly 30% of GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state#Effects).[^cite_note-Kenworthy-64][^cite_note-Bradley_et_al.-65]

The [New York Stock Exchange](21560.md) and [Nasdaq](21559.md) are the world's [largest stock exchanges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_stock_exchanges) by [market capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_capitalization) and volume of transactions.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-sfc.hk-67] U.S. capital markets are vast, with combined global equity and bond activity exceeding \$134.7 trillion.[^cite_note-68] It has ranked highly in global [venture capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venture_capital) as well as [research and development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_development) funding.[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] The U.S. has spent [around 3.46% of GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_by_research_and_development_spending) on cutting-edge research and development across various sectors of the economy since 2023.[^cite_note-71] Since the 1990s, [consumer spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_spending) has comprised over 60% to 70% of the U.S. economy.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-consumerecon-73] The U.S. is one of the top-performing economies in studies on the [ease of doing business index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ease_of_doing_business_index) and [global competitiveness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WEF_Global_Competitiveness_Report).[^cite_note-World_Economic_Forum-74]

## History

Main article: [Economic history of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_the_United_States)

### Colonial era and 18th century

Further information: [Mercantilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercantilism), [Colonialism](7299.md), and [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution)

The economic history of the United States traces back to British settlements along the [Eastern seaboard](89126.md) in the 17th and 18th centuries. After 1700, the United States gained population rapidly, and [imports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Import) as well as [exports](307592.md) grew along with it. [Africa](5334607.md), [Asia](689.md), and most frequently [Europe](9239.md), contributed to the trade of the colonies.[^cite_note-75] These [13 colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) [gained independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution) from the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) in the late 18th century[^cite_note-76] and quickly grew from colonial economies towards an economy focused on [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture).[^cite_note-77]

### 19th century

Further information: [Industrial Revolution in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution_in_the_United_States)

See also: [List of tariffs in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tariffs_in_the_United_States), [Protectionism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionism_in_the_United_States), [Tariff in United States history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariff_in_United_States_history), and [American System (economic plan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_System_\(economic_plan\))



[Washburn and Moen Manufacturing Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washburn_and_Moen_North_Works_District) in [Worcester, Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worcester,_Massachusetts), 1876

In 180 years, the United States grew to become a huge, [integrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_integration), and [industrialized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialisation) economy, which made up about a fifth of the [world economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_economy). In that process, the U.S. GDP per capita rose past that of many other countries, supplanting the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) at the top. The economy maintained high wages, attracting immigrants by the millions from all over the world.[^cite_note-78] In the 1820s and 1830s, mass production shifted much of the economy from artisans to factories. New government regulations strengthened patents.

Early in the 19th century, more than 80 percent of Americans engaged in farming. Most of the manufacturing centered on the first stages of the transformation of raw materials, with lumber and sawmills, textiles, and boots and shoes leading the way. The rich natural resources contributed to the rapid economic expansion of the nineteenth century. Ample land allowed the number of farmers to keep growing; but activity in manufacturing, services, transportation, and other sectors grew much faster, so that by 1860 the population was only about 50 percent rural, down from over 80 percent.[^cite_note-79]

In the 19th century, [recessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_recessions_in_the_United_States) frequently coincided with [financial crises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_crises). The [Panic of 1837](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic_of_1837) was followed by a five-year depression, marked by bank failures and unprecedented unemployment.[^cite_note-80] Because of the great changes in the economy over the centuries, it is difficult to compare the severity of modern recessions to that of early recessions.[^cite_note-81] Recessions after World War II appear to have been less severe than earlier recessions, but the reasons for this are unclear.[^cite_note-82]

### 20th century



[Oil wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_oil_in_California_through_1930) in [Los Angeles](18110.md), 1905



Assembling the [North American B-25 Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_B-25_Mitchell) at Kansas City, 1942



[McDonald's](2480627.md) restaurant in [Mount Pleasant, Iowa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Pleasant,_Iowa), 2008

At the beginning of the century, new [innovations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innovation) and improvements in existing innovations opened the door for improvements in the standard of living among American consumers. Many firms grew large by taking advantage of economies of scale and better communication to run nationwide operations. Concentration in these industries raised fears of monopolies that would drive prices higher and output lower, but many of these firms were cutting costs so fast that trends were towards lower prices and more output in these industries. Many workers shared the success of these large firms, which typically offered the highest wages in the world.[^cite_note-83]

The United States has been the world's largest national economy since around 1890, overtaking the United Kingdom in terms of GDP in 1872 and GDP per capita in 1905, adjusted for purchasing power parity (PPP).[^cite_note-84] For many years following the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) of the 1930s, when the danger of [recession](25382.md) appeared most serious, the government strengthened the economy by spending heavily itself or cutting taxes so that consumers would spend more and by fostering rapid growth in the money supply, which also encouraged more spending. Ideas about the best tools for [stabilizing the economy](318915.md) changed substantially between the 1930s and the 1980s. From the [New Deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Deal) era that began in 1933 to the [Great Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Society) initiatives of the 1960s, national policymakers relied principally on [fiscal policy](132645.md) to influence the economy.[^cite_note-85]

During the world wars of the 20th century, the United States fared better than the rest of the combatants because none of [World War I](4764461.md) and relatively little of [World War II](32927.md) were fought on American territory and neither war was fought in any of the 48 contiguous states. Yet, even in the United States, the wars meant sacrifice. During the peak of World War II activity, nearly 40 percent of U.S. GDP was devoted to war production. Decisions about large swaths of the economy were largely made for military purposes, and nearly all relevant inputs were allocated to the war effort. Many goods were rationed, prices and wages controlled, and many durable consumer goods were no longer produced. Large segments of the workforce were inducted into the military and paid half their wages; roughly half of those were sent into harm's way.[^cite_note-86]

The approach, advanced by British economist [John Maynard Keynes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Maynard_Keynes), gave elected officials a leading role in directing the economy since spending and taxes are controlled by the [U.S. president](24113.md) and [Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_the_United_States). The ["Baby Boom"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post%E2%80%93World_War_II_baby_boom) saw a dramatic increase in fertility in the period 1942–1957; it was caused by delayed marriages and childbearing during the depression years, a surge in prosperity, a demand for suburban single-family homes (as opposed to inner city apartments), and new optimism about the future. The boom peaked around 1957 and then began to fade.[^cite_note-Susan_Kellogg_1988-87] A period of high inflation, interest rates, and unemployment after 1973 weakened confidence in fiscal policy as a tool for regulating the overall pace of economic activity.[^cite_note-buchanan1977-88]

The U.S. economy grew by an [average](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_average_GDP_growth) of 3.8% from 1946 to 1973, while real [median household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_household_income) surged by 74% (or 2.1% a year).[^cite_note-hhes-89][^cite_note-90]

Since the 1970s, several [emerging countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_countries) have begun to close the economic gap with the United States. In most cases, this has been due to moving the manufacture of goods formerly made in the U.S. to countries where they could be made for sufficiently less money to cover the cost of shipping plus a higher profit. In other cases, some countries have gradually learned to produce the same products and services that previously only the U.S. and a few other countries could produce. Real [income growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wage_growth) in the U.S. has slowed. In the 1970s and 1980s, it was popular in the U.S. to believe that [Japan's economy](15578.md) would surpass that of the U.S., but this did not occur.[^cite_note-91]

### 21st century

Further information: [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession)



President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) with [automobile industry](23576520.md) leaders, 2017 (First Term)

The United States economy experienced a [recession in 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_2000s_recession) with an unusually slow jobs recovery, with the number of jobs not regaining the February 2001 level until January 2005.[^cite_note-Payems-92] This "jobless recovery" overlapped with the building of a [housing bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_housing_bubble) and arguably a wider debt bubble, as the ratio of household debt to GDP rose from a record level of 70% in Q1 2001 to 99% in Q1 2008. Homeowners were borrowing against their bubble-priced homes to fuel consumption, driving up their debt levels while providing an unsustainable boost to GDP. When housing prices began falling in 2006, the value of securities backed by mortgages fell dramatically, causing the equivalent of a [bank run](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_run) in the essentially unregulated [non-depository](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_banking) banking system, which had outgrown the traditional, regulated [depository banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depository_institution) system. Many mortgage companies and other non-depository banks (e.g., [investment banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investment_banking)) faced a worsening crisis in 2007–2008, with the [banking crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subprime_mortgage_crisis) peaking in [September 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), with the bankruptcy of [Lehman Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lehman_Brothers) and [bailouts](1469849.md) of several other financial institutions.[^cite_note-FCIC_1-93]

The [Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_George_W._Bush) (2001–2009) and [Obama administrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Barack_Obama) (2009–2017) applied banking [bailout programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troubled_Asset_Relief_Program) and [Keynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keynesian_economics) [stimulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Reinvestment_and_Recovery_Act) via high government deficits, while the [Federal Reserve](10819.md) maintained near-zero interest rates. These measures helped the economy recover, as households paid down debts in 2009–2012, the only years since 1947 where this occurred,[^cite_note-94] presenting a significant barrier to recovery.[^cite_note-FCIC_1-93] Real GDP regained its pre-crisis (late 2007) peak by 2011,[^cite_note-FRED_–_Real_GDP-95] household net worth by Q2 2012,[^cite_note-FRED_–_Household_Net_Worth-96] non-farm payroll jobs by May 2014,[^cite_note-Payems-92] and the unemployment rate by September 2015.[^cite_note-97] Each of these variables continued into post-recession record territory following those dates, with the U.S. recovery becoming the second longest on record in April 2018.[^cite_note-nyt2018-98]

The [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) occurred during the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), when GDP fell by 5.0% from the spring of 2008 to the spring of 2009. Other significant recessions took place in 1957–1958, when GDP fell 3.7% following the [1973 oil crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis), with a 3.1% fall from late 1973 to early 1975, and in the 1981–1982 recession, when GDP dropped by 2.9%.[^cite_note-gcn-99][^cite_note-cnn1-100] Recent, mild recessions have included the 1990–1991 downturn, when output fell by 1.3%, and the 2001 recession, in which GDP slid by 0.3%; the 2001 downturn lasted just eight months.[^cite_note-cnn1-100] The most vigorous, sustained periods of growth, on the other hand, took place from early 1961 to mid-1969, with an expansion of 53% (5.1% a year), from mid-1991 to late 2000, at 43% (3.8% a year), and from late 1982 to mid-1990, at 37% (4% a year).[^cite_note-gcn-99]

Debt held by the public, a measure of [national debt](695460.md), has risen throughout the 21st century. Rising from 31% in 2000 to 52% in 2009, reaching 77% of GDP in 2017, and exceeding 120% in 2024, the U.S. ranked 43rd highest in debt out of 207 countries.[^cite_note-101]

#### COVID-19 pandemic

Main article: [Economic impact of the COVID-19 pandemic in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States)

In the first two quarters of 2020 amid [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s presidency,[^cite_note-CNBC_Q3-102] the U.S. economy suffered major setbacks beginning in March 2020, due to the [novel coronavirus](62750956.md) and having to "shut-down" major sectors of the American economy.[^cite_note-103] As of March 2020, US exports of automobiles and industrial machines had plummeted as a result of the worldwide pandemic.[^cite_note-104] [Social distancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_distancing) measures which took effect in March 2020, and which negatively impacted the demand for goods and services, resulted in the US [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP) declining at a [4.8% annualized rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade-to-GDP_ratio) in the first quarter, the steepest pace of contraction in output since the fourth quarter of 2008.[^cite_note-105] US retails sales dropped a record 8.7% in March alone. The US airline industry had also been hit hard, seeing a sharp decline in its revenues.[^cite_note-106] The [COVID-19 recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_recession) has been widely described as the most severe global economic downturn since the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) and "far worse" than the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession).[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110]



[Midtown Manhattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Manhattan), the world's largest [central business district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_business_district)

[^cite_note-111]

In May 2020, [CNN](62028.md) gave an analysis based on unemployment data that the US economy was perhaps the worst that it had been since the 1930s.[^cite_note-112] By May 8, the US had reached a record 14.7 percent unemployment, with 20.5 million jobs lost in April.[^cite_note-113] The Chairman of the [US Federal Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Federal_Reserve), [Jerome Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerome_Powell), warned that it may take "an extended time" before the US economy fully recovers from weak economic growth, due to the pandemic, and that in the foreseeable future the US can expect "low productivity growth and stagnant incomes".[^cite_note-114] By May 31, 2020, more than forty million Americans had filed for unemployment benefits.[^cite_note-115]

By June 2020, the slump in US continental flights due to the coronavirus pandemic had resulted in the US government temporarily halting service of fifteen US airlines to 75 domestic airports.[^cite_note-116] *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported on June 10, 2020, that "the United States budget deficit grew to a record \$1.88 trillion for the first eight months of this [fiscal year](5752099.md)."[^cite_note-117]

The US economy recovered from the COVID-19 pandemic in 2021, growing by 5.7%, which was its best performance since [Ronald Reagan](25433.md)'s presidency (1981–1989).[^cite_note-118]

2021–2022 marked a historical [inflation surge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%932022_inflation_surge) in the United States, with the [Consumer Price Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_Price_Index) inflation rate hitting 9.1% higher in June 2022 than June 2021, constituting a 41-year high inflation rate with critics blaming the [Federal Reserve](10819.md) among other factors.[^cite_note-119] Inflation rate reached 4.9% in April 2023, which was roughly 3% above the Federal Reserve's 2% target rate.[^cite_note-120]

According to a 2024 report put out by [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution), the American economy fared better following the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic as compared to the Great Recession, also outpacing the recovery of other countries in the G10. In contrast, both Republican and Democratic leaders provided unprecedented fiscal support through relief bills in 2020 and 2021 to preserve economic activity. Researchers found the preservation of payroll and direct support to households mitigated much of the long-term impact felt following the 2008 crisis, and the supply chain disruptions were more to blame for the inflation that occurred.[^cite_note-121]

#### Post-COVID economy

| \[icon] | This section **needs expansion**. You can help by [adding missing information](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Economy_of_the_United_States\&action=edit\&section=). *(July 2026)* |
| --- | --- |

On a global scale, the US recovered from COVID's economic drawbacks better than other countries. This includes outpacing the recovery of other economic groups like the [G10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G10_country) and [Eurozone](184391.md), with private consumption rising at a higher pace.[^cite_note-122] By September 2025, inflation had fallen to 2.9%.[^cite_note-123]

The post-COVID US economy has been described as a "[K-shaped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recession_shapes)" economy, or an economy in which wealthy income earners are benefiting from the stock market and rising property values, while lower income earners are having more difficulty making ends meet.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125]

The pandemic has been determined as a major contributor to the increased cost of housing in the United States, according to a study published in the [National Library of Medicine.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institutes_of_Health)[^cite_note-\:1-126] Prior to the pandemic, home ownership rates were already falling due to continued challenges caused by the 2008 recession. The pandemic exacerbated the crisis due to layoffs, rent moratoriums, and increasing foreclosures as the result in lost income. People chose to leave highly populated areas for more rural communities. More people began working from home, allowing for more flexibility in where they lived. The interest rate fell on 30-year mortgages, further increasing the demand for homes.[^cite_note-\:1-126] The result was rapidly increasing housing costs. The most impacted have been minorities and people with disabilities.[^cite_note-\:1-126]

According to a report released July 13, 2026 by the [Federal Reserve Bank of Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Bank_of_Cleveland), the earnings gap between Black and Hispanic men and White men narrowed following the COVID-19 recession to its lowest point in 25 years in 2022. Black and Hispanic men also experienced a stronger employment recovery than that of White men for the same time period. The shift coincided with, but is not fully attributed to, a slight increase in the number of Black and Hispanic males that obtained both a high school diploma and Bachelors degree.[^cite_note-127]

In 2025, [Indeed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indeed) research stated new job listings had fallen below pre-pandemic levels. [Challenger, Gray & Christmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Challenger,_Gray_&_Christmas), a global outplacement firm, stated planned layoffs totaled over 1.1 million that November, which was 54% more than in 2024 for the same time period.[^cite_note-128] By mid 2026, 26% of unemployed people had been jobless for longer than six months. Many turned to starting their own business rather than seeking traditional employment.[^cite_note-129] Consumer sentiment had reached a low comparable to that of 2022, according to [The University of Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Michigan)’s consumer sentiment survey. Another survey by [KeyBank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KeyBank) stated that 35% of 1,000 Americans surveyed were seeking some sort of secondary income through self employment.[^cite_note-\:2-130] According to business formation reports from [Registered Agents Inc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Registered_Agents_Inc.), the number of formations has increased since the initial recovery of the pandemic.[^cite_note-131] The number of businesses formed reached a record monthly high in March 2026, with 624,915 business formations nationwide.[^cite_note-\:2-130] *[The New York Times](30680.md)* and [*Inc.*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inc._\(magazine\)) reported that part of the increase in business formations is likely due to the availability of artificial intelligence and its role in simplifying the process of getting started, either by providing answers to questions, or completing tasks that previously required the hiring of employees.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133]

#### 2026 Iran war

In April 2026, the [U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Labor_Statistics) reported a 0.9% increase in the Consumer Price Index (CPI) for March 2026, based on the latest data; however, a government shutdown in late 2025 had disrupted the data collection and analysis process. The price index for heating oil rose by 44.2% compared to the previous year, while energy costs increased by 12.5%. Food costs also rose by 2.7% through March 2026. Trading Economics has forecast an inflation rate of approximately 3.5% for the remainder of 2026.[^cite_note-134]

In late September 2026, Federal Reserve Board member [Michael Barr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Barr_\(U.S._official\)) stated, "The combination of tariffs and a prolonged war with Iran has caused us to 'deviate from our original path toward the 2% inflation target.'" Consumer spending is expected to rise 0.8% in August, partly due to higher gas prices. In July, it only rose 0.2%. Bank of America noted strong spending despite increased energy costs. Debt and credit card spending grew 6.9% year-over-year, largely from a 26.5% rise in gasoline, while spending excluding gas increased 5.7%.[^cite_note-135]According to the [Joint Economic Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Congressional_Joint_Economic_Committee), energy price inflation rose to 16.28 percent from August 2025 to August 2206.[^cite_note-136]

## GDP

Main article: [Economic history of the United States § 1790–2006 GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_the_United_States#1790%E2%80%932006_GDP)



United States real quarterly GDP (annualized)



U.S. cumulative real (inflation-adjusted) GDP growth by US president (from Reagan to Obama)

[^cite_note-138]



Private sector workers earnings compared to GDP \
&#x20;Private sector workers made \~\$2 trillion or about 29.6% of all money earned in Q3 2023 (before taxes)&#x20;

&#x20; Quarterly [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP) not Annualized

&#x20; Private Sector Workers Total Earnings

U.S. [nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominal_GDP) was estimated at \$31.856 trillion in the first quarter of 2026, the largest in the world.[^cite_note-139] Annualized, nominal GDP reached \$20.1 trillion in Q1 2018, the first time it exceeded \$20 trillion. About 70% of U.S. GDP is personal consumption, with business investment 18%, government 17% (federal, state and local but excluding transfer payments such as Social Security, which is in consumption), and net exports a negative 3% due to the U.S. trade deficit.[^cite_note-140]

Real [gross domestic product](12594.md) increased at an annualized rate of 1.5% in the second quarter of 2026, following an annualized increase of 2.1% in the first quarter.[^cite_note-141]

Real [gross domestic product](12594.md), a measure of both production and income, grew by 2.3% in 2017, vs. 1.5% in 2016 and 2.9% in 2015. Real GDP grew at a quarterly annualized rate of 2.2% in Q1 2018, 4.2% in Q2 2018, 3.4% in Q3 2018, and 2.2% in Q4 2018; the Q2 rate was the best growth rate since Q3 2014, and the overall yearly GDP growth of 2.9% in 2018 was the best performance of the economy in a decade.[^cite_note-Real_GDP-142] In 2020, the growth rate of the GDP started to drop as a result of the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), resulting in the GDP decreasing at an annual rate of −5.0% in Q1 2020 and −32.9% in Q2 2020, respectively.[^cite_note-143]

As of 2014, [China](5405.md) passed the U.S. as the largest economy in GDP (PPP) terms, measured at purchasing power parity conversion rates. The U.S. had the highest GDP (PPP) figures for more than a century prior to that milestone; China has more than tripled the U.S. growth rate for each of the past 40 years. As of 2017, the [European Union](9317.md) as an aggregate had a GDP roughly 5% larger than the U.S., although the former is a [political union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_union), not a country. The United States', however, remained the world's largest economy with the highest nominal GDP.[^cite_note-144]

Real GDP per capita (measured in 2009 dollars) was \$52,444 in 2017 and has been growing each year since 2010. It grew 3.0% per year on average in the 1960s, 2.1% in the 1970s, 2.4% in the 1980s, 2.2% in the 1990s, 0.7% in the 2000s, and 0.9% from 2010 to 2017.[^cite_note-145] Reasons for slower growth since 2000 are debated by economists and may include aging demographics, slower population and growth in labor force, slower productivity growth, reduced corporate investment, greater income inequality reducing demand, lack of major innovations, and reduced labor power.[^cite_note-146] The U.S. ranked 20th out of 220 countries in [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP_per_capita) in 2017.[^cite_note-147] Among the modern U.S. Presidents, [Bill Clinton](3356.md) had the highest cumulative percent real GDP increase during his two terms, [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) second, and [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) third.[^cite_note-Real_GDP-142]

The development of the nation's GDP according to [World Bank](36752.md):[^cite_note-148] U.S. real GDP grew by an average of 1.7% from 2000 to the first half of 2014, a rate around half the historical average up to 2000.[^cite_note-bea.gov-149]

## Employment

Further information: [List of largest United States–based employers globally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_United_States%E2%80%93based_employers_globally) and [List of U.S. states by employment rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_employment_rate)

See also: [JOLTS report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JOLTS_report) and [Lists of companies of the United States by state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_companies_of_the_United_States_by_state)

[^cite_note-153]



Panel chart illustrates nine key economic variables measured annually in 2014–2017. The years 2014–2016 were during President Obama's second term, while 2017 was during President Trump's term. Refer to citations on detail page.

There were approximately 160.4 million people in the U.S. labor force in 2017, the fourth largest labor force in the world behind China, India, and the European Union.[^cite_note-154] The government (federal, state and local) employed 22 million in 2010.[^cite_note-Mcfeatters-155] [Small businesses](276495.md) are the nation's largest employer, representing 37% of American workers.[^cite_note-sba.gov-156] The second-largest share of employment belongs to large businesses employing 36% of the U.S. workforce.[^cite_note-sba.gov-156] [White collar workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-collar_worker) comprise 44% of the workforce as of 2022, up from 34% in 2000.[^cite_note-157]

The nation's [private sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_sector) employs 85% of working Americans. [Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector) accounts for 14% of all U.S. workers. Over 99% of all private employing organizations in the U.S. are small businesses.[^cite_note-sba.gov-156] The 30 million small businesses in the U.S. account for 64% of newly created jobs (those created minus those lost).[^cite_note-sba.gov-156] Jobs in small businesses accounted for 70% of those created in the last decade.[^cite_note-158]

The proportion of Americans employed by small business versus large business has remained relatively the same year by year as some small businesses become large businesses and just over half of small businesses survive for more than five years.[^cite_note-sba.gov-156] Among large businesses, several of the largest companies and employers in the world are American companies. Among them is [Walmart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walmart), which is both the largest company and the largest [private sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_sector) employer in the world. Walmart employs 2.1 million people worldwide and 1.4 million in the U.S. alone.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160]



US Census Bureau (number of employees per business)



Since the 1970s there has been a decoupling of U.S. wage gains from worker productivity.

[^cite_note-161]

There are nearly thirty million small businesses in the U.S.. Minorities such as [Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans), African Americans, Asian Americans, and Native Americans (35% of the country's population),[^cite_note-162] own 4.1 million of the nation's businesses. Minority-owned businesses generate almost \$700 billion in revenue, and they employ almost five million workers in the U.S.[^cite_note-sba.gov-156][^cite_note-163]

Americans have the highest average [employee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_average_wage) income among OECD nations.[^cite_note-OECD-164] The median household income in the U.S. as of 2008 is \$52,029.[^cite_note-165] About 284,000 working people in the U.S. have two full-time jobs and 7.6 million have part-time ones in addition to their full-time employments.[^cite_note-Mcfeatters-155] Out of all [working individuals in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_class_in_the_United_States), 12% belong to a labor union and most union members work for the government.[^cite_note-Mcfeatters-155] The decline of [union membership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_unions_in_the_United_States) in the U.S. over the last several decades parallels that of labor's share of the economy.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] The World Bank ranks the United States first in the ease of hiring and firing workers.[^cite_note-EDBI-169]

The United States is the only advanced economy that does not [legally guarantee its workers paid vacation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_statutory_minimum_employment_leave_by_country) or [paid sick days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sick_leave#United_States), and is one of just a few countries in the world without [paid family leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paid_family_leave) as a [legal right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_right), with the others being [Papua New Guinea](22943.md), [Suriname](26828.md) and [Liberia](17791.md).[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] In 2014 and again in 2020, the [International Trade Union Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Trade_Union_Confederation) graded the U.S. a 4 out of 5+, its third-lowest score, on the subject of [workers' rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_rights).[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] Similarly, a 2023 study published by [Oxfam](19698439.md) found that the United States ranks among the worst among developed countries for [labor protections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_protection).[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] Some scholars, including business theorist [Jeffrey Pfeffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Pfeffer) and political scientist Daniel Kinderman, posit that contemporary employment practices in the United States relating to the increased performance pressure from management, and the hardships imposed on employees such as toxic working environments, [precarity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precarity), and long hours, could be responsible for 120,000 excess deaths annually, making the workplace the fifth leading cause of death in the United States.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179]

### Unemployment

Main articles: [Unemployment in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment_in_the_United_States) and [List of U.S. states and territories by unemployment rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_unemployment_rate)



U1-U6 unemployment rate

As of December 2017, the [unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment) rate in the U.S. was 4.1%[^cite_note-180] or 6.6 million people.[^cite_note-181] The government's broader U-6 unemployment rate, which includes the part-time [underemployed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underemployment), was 8.1%[^cite_note-182] or 8.2 million people. These figures were calculated with a civilian labor force of approximately 160.6 million people,[^cite_note-research.stlouisfed.org-183] relative to a U.S. population of approximately 327 million people.[^cite_note-184]

Between 2009 and 2010, following the Great Recession, the emerging problem of [jobless recoveries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jobless_recovery) resulted in record levels of [long-term unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-term_unemployment) with more than six million workers looking for work for more than six months as of January 2010. This particularly affected older workers.[^cite_note-Goodman-185] A year after the recession ended in June 2009, immigrants gained 656,000 jobs in the U.S., while U.S.-born workers lost more than a million jobs, due in part to an aging country (relatively more white retirees) and demographic shifts.[^cite_note-186] In April 2010, the official unemployment rate was 9.9%, but the government's broader [U-6 unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment#United_States_Bureau_of_Labor_statistics) rate was 17.1%.[^cite_note-187] Between February 2008 and February 2010, the number of people working part-time for economic reasons (i.e., would prefer to work full-time) increased by 4.0 million to 8.8 million, an 83% increase in part-time workers during the two-year period.[^cite_note-EconPost-188]

By 2013, although the unemployment rate had fallen below 8%, the record proportion of long term unemployed and continued decreasing household income remained indicative of a jobless recovery.[^cite_note-SchwartzJobless-189] However, the number of payroll jobs returned to its pre-recession (November 2007) level by May 2014 as the economy recovered.[^cite_note-190]

After being higher in the post-war period, the U.S. unemployment rate fell below the rising [eurozone](184391.md) unemployment rate in the mid-1980s and has remained significantly lower almost continuously since.[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] In 1955, 55% of Americans worked in services, between 30% and 35% in industry, and between 10% and 15% in [agriculture](242778.md). By 1980, over 65% were employed in services, between 25% and 30% in industry, and less than 5% in agriculture.[^cite_note-194] [Male unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Male_unemployment) continued to be significantly higher than those of females (at 9.8% vs. 7.5% in 2009). The unemployment among Caucasians continues being much lower than those for African-Americans (at 8.5% vs. 15.8% also in 2009).[^cite_note-CPS06052009-195]

The [youth unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_unemployment) rate was 18.5% in July 2009, the highest rate in that month since 1948.[^cite_note-196] The unemployment rate of young African Americans was 28.2% in May 2013.[^cite_note-197]

The unemployment rate reached an all-time high of 14.7% in April 2020 before falling back to 11.1% in June 2020. Due to the effects of the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md), Q2 GDP in the US fell 32.9% in 2020.[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200] The unemployment rate continued its rapid decline falling to 3.9% in 2021.[^cite_note-201] It reached 3.7% in May 2023.[^cite_note-202]

In 2025, the United States labor market experienced a notable shift characterized by stagnant hiring and increased layoffs. According to Reuters, between January and September 2025, U.S. companies announced approximately 950,000 job cuts, with the technology, retail, and government sectors accounting for the largest share. Analysts described this trend as a transition from a “no hire, no fire” environment to a “no hire, more fire” phase, reflecting rising corporate caution and slowing economic momentum.[^cite_note-203]

Economic data released in late 2025 indicated emerging signs of a slowdown in the U.S. economy. The unemployment rate rose to 4.6% in November, its highest level since 2021, while job creation slowed, with only 64,000 jobs added, down from previous months.[^cite_note-204]

Partial data also showed a decline in employment in October, with analysts noting weaker hiring momentum amid elevated inflation and higher borrowing costs. At the same time, retail sales remained flat at the start of the holiday season, raising concerns about consumer spending, which accounts for a large share of U.S. economic activity.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206]

The US economy began 2026 still in a "high vacancy" position on the [Beveridge curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beveridge_curve), which looks at the relationship between job openings and unemployment.[^cite_note-Beveridge2026-207] By the end of January 2026, hiring and layoff rates continued to be low, allowing unemployment to stay range-bound.[^cite_note-Beveridge2026-207] The low number of job openings, however, means that if layoffs were to increase then unemployment would comparatively increase as well.[^cite_note-Beveridge2026-207]

### Employment by sector

See also: [Employment by major industry sector in United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_United_States%E2%80%93based_employers_globally#Employment_by_major_industry_sector)

U.S. employment, as estimated in 2012, is divided into 79.7% in the service sector, 19.2% in the manufacturing sector, and 1.1% in the agriculture sector.[^cite_note-208]

U.S. family pre-tax income and net worth distribution for 2013 and 2016, from the Federal Reserve [Survey of Consumer Finances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survey_of_Consumer_Finances)

[^cite_note-fed2017sept-209]

### Income measures

The American daily per person [median income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_income) was \$70 ([PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity)) in 2023, one of the highest in the world.[^cite_note-r236-210] Real (i.e., inflation-adjusted) median household income, a good measure of middle-class income, was \$59,039 in 2016, a record level. However, it was just above the previous record set in 1998, indicating the purchasing power of middle-class family income has been stagnant or down for much of the past twenty years.[^cite_note-211] According to one analysis middle-class incomes in the United States fell into a tie with those in Canada in 2010, and may have fallen behind by 2014, while several other advanced economies have closed the gap in recent years.[^cite_note-212]

Americans have the highest average [household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Household_income) among OECD nations.[^cite_note-Household_Income-213][^cite_note-OECD-164] During 2013, employee compensation was \$8.969 trillion, while gross private investment totals \$2.781 trillion.[^cite_note-Fed_Z.1-214]

[Income inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_inequality) has fluctuated considerably in the United States since measurements began around 1915, moving in an arc between peaks in the 1920s and 2000s, with a lower level of inequality from approximately 1950-1980 (a period named the [Great Compression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Compression)), followed by increasing inequality, in what has been coined as the [great divergence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Divergence_\(inequality\)).

The U.S. has the highest level of income inequality among its (post-industrialized) peers.[^cite_note-Income_inequality_in_the_United_States_upi.com-215] When measured for all households, U.S. income inequality is comparable to other developed countries before taxes and transfers, but is among the highest after taxes and transfers, meaning the U.S. shifts relatively less income from higher income households to lower income households. In 2016, average market income was \$15,600 for the lowest [quintile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantile) and \$280,300 for the highest quintile. The degree of inequality accelerated within the top quintile, with the top 1% at \$1.8 million, approximately 30 times the \$59,300 income of the middle quintile.[^cite_note-Income_inequality_in_the_United_States_CBO_2016-216]

The economic and political impacts of inequality may include slower GDP growth, reduced [income mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_mobility), higher poverty rates, greater use of [household debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Household_debt), leading to an increased risk of financial crises, and [political polarization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_polarization).[^cite_note-Income_inequality_in_the_United_States_Krueger2012-217][^cite_note-Income_inequality_in_the_United_States_SA2020-218] Causes of inequality may include [executive compensation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_compensation) increasing relative to the average worker, [financialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financialization), greater [industry concentration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_concentration), lower [unionization rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_density), lower effective tax rates on higher incomes, and technology changes that reward higher educational attainment.[^cite_note-Income_inequality_in_the_United_States_NYTPorter2013-219]

Measurement is debated, as inequality measures vary significantly, for example, across datasets[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221] or whether the measurement is taken based on cash compensation (market income) or after taxes and [transfer payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_payment). The [Gini coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient) is a widely accepted statistic that allows comparisons across jurisdictions, with 0 indicating perfect equality and 1 indicating maximum inequality. Further, various public and private data sets measure those incomes, e.g., from the [Congressional Budget Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office) (CBO),[^cite_note-Income_inequality_in_the_United_States_CBO_2016-216] the Internal Revenue Service, and Census.[^cite_note-Income_inequality_in_the_United_States_PSZ_NatDistAcct-222] According to the Census Bureau, income inequality reached then record levels in 2018, with a Gini of 0.485,[^cite_note-223] Since then the Census Bureau have given values of 0.488 in 2020 and 0.494 in 2021, per pre-tax money income.[^cite_note-224] For 2025, the Census Bureau reported a Gini index of 0.490 for pretax money income, a change from 2024 that was not statistically significant.[^cite_note-225]

U.S. [tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax_in_the_United_States) and transfer policies are [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_tax) and therefore reduce effective income inequality, as rates of tax generally increase as taxable income increases. As a group, the lowest earning [workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_class_in_the_United_States), especially those with dependents, pay no income taxes and may actually receive a small subsidy from the federal government (from child credits and the [Earned Income Tax Credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earned_Income_Tax_Credit)).[^cite_note-Income_inequality_in_the_United_States_CBO_2010-226] The 2016 U.S. Gini coefficient was .59 based on market income; it was reduced to .42 after taxes and transfers, according to [Congressional Budget Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office) (CBO) figures. The top 1% share of market income rose from 9.6% in 1979 to a peak of 20.7% in 2007, before falling to 17.5% by 2016. After taxes and transfers, these figures were 7.4%, 16.6%, and 12.5%, respectively.[^cite_note-Income_inequality_in_the_United_States_CBO_2016-216]

### Household net worth and wealth inequality

Net personal wealth in the U.S. since 1962



The average personal wealth of people in the top 1% is more than a thousand times that of people in bottom 50%.

[^cite_note-WID_through_2021-227]



The logarithmic scale shows how wealth has increased for all percentile groups, though moreso for wealthier people.

[^cite_note-WID_through_2021-227]

As of Q4 2017, total household net worth in the United States was a record \$99 trillion, an increase of \$5.2 trillion from 2016. This increase reflects both stock market and housing price gains. This measure has been setting records since Q4 2012.[^cite_note-228] If divided evenly, the \$99 trillion represents an average of \$782,000 per household (for about 126.2 million households) or \$302,000 per person. However, median household net worth (i.e., half of the families above and below this level) was \$97,300 in 2016. The bottom 25% of families had a median net worth of zero, while the 25th to 50th percentile had a median net worth of \$40,000.[^cite_note-229]

Wealth inequality is more unequal than income inequality, with the top 1% households owning approximately 42% of the net worth in 2012, versus 24% in 1979.[^cite_note-230] According to a September 2017 report by the Federal Reserve, wealth inequality is at record highs; the top 1% controlled 38.6% of the country's wealth in 2016.[^cite_note-231] The [Boston Consulting Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Consulting_Group) posited in June 2017 report that 1% of the Americans will control 70% of country's wealth by 2021.[^cite_note-232]

The top 10% wealthiest possess 80% of all financial assets.[^cite_note-hurst34-233] [Wealth inequality in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_inequality_in_the_United_States) is greater than in most developed countries other than Sweden.[^cite_note-GlobalWealthRpt-234] [Inherited wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inherited_wealth) may help explain why many Americans who have become rich may have had a "substantial head start".[^cite_note-Salon-20140324-235][^cite_note-ECO-20140318-236] In September 2012, according to the [Institute for Policy Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Policy_Studies), "over 60 percent" of the [Forbes richest 400 Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_400) "grew up in substantial privilege".[^cite_note-OW-20120924-237] Median household wealth fell 35% in the U.S., from \$106,591 to \$68,839 between 2005 and 2011, due to the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession), but has since recovered as indicated above.[^cite_note-238]

About 30% of the entire world's millionaire population resides in the United States (as of 2009).[^cite_note-WorldWealthReport2010-239] The [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md) estimated in 2008 that there were 16,600,000 millionaires in the U.S.[^cite_note-240] Furthermore, 34% of the world's billionaires are American (in 2011).[^cite_note-forbes.com-241][^cite_note-242]

### Home ownership

Further information: [Home-ownership in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home-ownership_in_the_United_States) and [Housing in the United States](66095208.md)



Cost of housing by State

The U.S. home ownership rate in Q1 2018 was 64.2%, well below the all-time peak of 69.2% set in Q4 2004 during a [housing bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_housing_bubble). Millions of homes were lost to foreclosure during the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) of 2007–2009, bringing the ownership rate to a trough of 62.9% in Q2 2016. The average ownership rate from 1965 to 2017 was 65.3%.[^cite_note-243]

The average home in the United States has more than 700 square feet per person (65 square meters), which is 50%–100% more than the average in other high-income countries. Similarly, ownership rates of gadgets and amenities are relatively high compared to other countries.[^cite_note-Rector-Johnson-244][^cite_note-Rector-245][^cite_note-246]

It was reported by Pew Research Center in 2016 that, for the first time in 130 years, Americans aged 18 to 34 are more likely to live with their parents than in any other housing situation.[^cite_note-247]

In one study by ATTOM Data Solutions, in 70% of the counties surveyed, homes are increasingly unaffordable for the average U.S. worker.[^cite_note-248]

As of 2018, the number of U.S. citizens residing in their vehicles increased in major cities with significantly higher than average housing costs such as [Los Angeles](18110.md), [Portland](23503.md) and [San Francisco](49728.md).[^cite_note-249][^cite_note-250]

According to [CNBC](223441.md), the median sale price for a U.S. home in 2017 was US\$199,200.[^cite_note-251] By February 2023, the median U.S. home sale price grew to US\$392,000 according to [Statista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statista).[^cite_note-252] The US has a country-wide housing shortage caused by insufficient housing construction (which declined severely after the 2008 [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession)), and has caused rents and home prices to rise to increasingly unaffordable levels, with one estimate of the shortage being 3.8 million units in 2019, with this shortage having gotten worse during and since the pandemic.[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254]

As of January 2024, in roughly half of cities in the U.S., workers need incomes of \$100,000 or more in order to purchase a home as a result of rising housing prices and interest rate hikes.[^cite_note-255]

### Profits and wages



Wages in the United States&#x20;

&#x20; [Nominal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominalism) wages

&#x20; [Adjusted for inflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjusted-for-inflation) wages

See also: [List of U.S. states and territories by median wage and mean wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_median_wage_and_mean_wage)

[Real wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_wages) (wages adjusted for inflation) for most workers in the United States and median incomes have either declined or remained stagnant for the last twenty to forty years.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259] A 2020 microanalysis demonstrated that in the preceding four decades labor's share of national output declined while over the same period the profit share of the same output increased.[^cite_note-260]

In 1970, [wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wage) represented more than 51% of the U.S. GDP and profits were less than 5%. But by 2013, wages had fallen to 44% of the economy, while profits had more than doubled to 11%.[^cite_note-DThompson-261] Inflation-adjusted ("real") per capita [disposable personal income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disposable_and_discretionary_income) rose steadily in the U.S. from 1945 to 2008, but has since remained generally level.[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263]

In 2005, median personal income for those over the age of 18 ranged from \$3,317 for an unemployed, married [Asian American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_American) female[^cite_note-264] to \$55,935 for a full-time, year-round employed Asian American male.[^cite_note-265] According to the U.S. Census men tended to have higher income than women while Asians and [Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_American) earned more than [African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American) and [Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans). The overall median personal income for all individuals over the age of 18 was \$24,062[^cite_note-266] (\$32,140 for those age 25 or above) in the year 2005.[^cite_note-267]

As a reference point, the minimum wage rate in 2009 and 2017 was \$7.25 per hour or \$15,080 for the 2080 hours in a typical work year. The minimum wage is a little more than the poverty level for a single person unit and about 50% of the [poverty level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_the_United_States) for a family of four.

According to an October 2014 report by the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center), [real wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_wages) have been flat or falling for the last five decades for most U.S. workers, regardless of job growth.[^cite_note-268] Bloomberg reported in July 2018 that real GDP per capita has grown substantially since the Great Recession.[^cite_note-269]

An August 2017 survey by [CareerBuilder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CareerBuilder) found that eight out of ten U.S. workers live paycheck to paycheck. CareerBuilder spokesman Mike Erwin blamed "stagnant wages and the rising cost of everything from education to many consumer goods".[^cite_note-270] According to a survey by the federal [Consumer Financial Protection Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_Financial_Protection_Bureau) on the financial well-being of U.S. citizens, roughly half have trouble paying bills, and more than one third have faced hardships such as not being able to afford a place to live, running out of food, or not having enough money to pay for medical care.[^cite_note-271] According to journalist and author [Alissa Quart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alissa_Quart), the cost of living is rapidly outpacing the growth of salaries and wages, including those for traditionally secure professions such as teaching. She writes that "middle-class life is now 30% more expensive than it was 20 years ago."[^cite_note-272]

In February 2019, the [Federal Reserve Bank of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Bank_of_New_York) reported that seven million U.S. citizens are three months or more behind on their car payments, setting a record. This is considered a red flag by economists, that Americans are struggling to pay bills in spite of a low unemployment rate.[^cite_note-273] A May 2019 poll conducted by [NPR](29697232.md) found that among rural Americans, 40% struggle to pay for healthcare, food and housing, and 49% could not pay cash for a \$1,000 emergency, and would instead choose to borrow in order to pay for such an unexpected emergency expense.[^cite_note-274] Some experts assert that the US has experienced a "two-tier recovery", which has benefitted 60% of the population, while the other 40% on the "lower tier" have been struggling to pay bills as the result of stagnant wages, increases in the cost of housing, education and healthcare, and growing debts.[^cite_note-275]

A 2021 study by the [National Low Income Housing Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Low_Income_Housing_Coalition) found that workers would have to make at least \$24.90 an hour to be able to afford (meaning 30% of a person's income or less) renting a standard two-bedroom home or \$20.40 for a one-bedroom home anywhere in the US. The former is 3.4 times higher than the current federal minimum wage.[^cite_note-276]

The [USCB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USCB) reported in September 2023 that incomes fell last year by 2.3% from 2021, which is the third consecutive year incomes have declined.[^cite_note-277]

According to a 2026 analysis by Federal Reserve economists, [American workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_class_in_the_United_States)' share of national income has fallen to the lowest level since 1947, with corporations and wealthy investors taking an ever greater portion.[^cite_note-278]

### Poverty

Main article: [Poverty in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_the_United_States)



Number in poverty and poverty rate: 1959 to 2016. United States.

Starting in the 1980s [relative poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relative_poverty) rates have consistently exceeded those of other wealthy nations, though analyses using a common data set for comparisons tend to find that the U.S. has a lower absolute poverty rate by market income than most other wealthy nations.[^cite_note-National_Research_Council-279] [Extreme poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_poverty) in the United States, meaning households living on less than \$2 per day before government benefits, doubled from 1996 levels to 1.5 million households in 2011, including 2.8 million children.[^cite_note-NatlPovertyCtr-280] In 2013, [child poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_poverty) reached record high levels, with 16.7 million children living in [food insecure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famine_scales#Combined_intensity_and_magnitude_scales) households, about 35% more than 2007 levels.[^cite_note-WalkerBBC-281] As of 2015, 44 percent of children in the United States live with low-income families.[^cite_note-282]

In 2016, 12.7% of the U.S. population [lived in poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_the_United_States), down from 13.5% in 2015. The poverty rate rose from 12.5% in 2007 before the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) to a 15.1% peak in 2010, before falling back to just above the 2007 level. In the 1959–1962 period, the poverty rate was over 20%, but declined to the all-time low of 11.1% in 1973 following the [War on Poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_Poverty) begun during the Lyndon Johnson presidency.[^cite_note-283] In June 2016, The IMF warned the United States that its high poverty rate needs to be tackled urgently.[^cite_note-284]



[Wealth inequality in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_inequality_in_the_United_States) increased from 1989 to 2013.

[^cite_note-285]

The population in extreme-poverty neighborhoods rose by one third from 2000 to 2009.[^cite_note-Concentrated_Poverty-286] People living in such neighborhoods tend to suffer from inadequate access to quality education; higher crime rates; higher rates of physical and psychological ailment; limited access to credit and wealth accumulation; higher prices for goods and services; and constrained access to job opportunities.[^cite_note-Concentrated_Poverty-286] As of 2013, 44% of America's poor are considered to be in "deep poverty", with an income 50% or more below the government's official poverty line.[^cite_note-287]

According to the US [Department of Housing and Urban Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development)'s Annual Homeless Assessment Report, as of 2024 there were around 771,480 [homeless people in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homelessness_in_the_United_States) on a given night, or about 23 of every 10,000 people.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] Almost two thirds stayed in an emergency shelter or transitional housing program and the other third were living on the street, in an abandoned building, or another place not meant for human habitation. About 1.56 million people, or about 0.5% of the U.S. population, used an emergency shelter or a transitional housing program between October 1, 2008, and September 30, 2009.[^cite_note-HUDhomeless2009-290] Around 44% of homeless people are employed.[^cite_note-291] Homelessness increased from 2016 to 2020, along with deaths among the homeless population.[^cite_note-292]

A homeless camp under a highway bridge in New Orleans, LA

A homeless camp in New Orleans, March 2023

The United States has one of the least extensive social safety nets in the developed world, reducing both relative poverty and absolute poverty by [considerably less than the mean for wealthy nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare's_effect_on_poverty).[^cite_note-Sme-293][^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297] Poverty rates in the US are higher and the conditions of those in poverty more extreme compared to other Western countries.[^cite_note-298] Some experts posit that those in poverty live in conditions rivaling the [developing world](78449.md).[^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300] A May 2018 report by the U.N. Special Rapporteur on extreme poverty and human rights found that over five million people in the United States live "in 'Third World' conditions".[^cite_note-301] Poverty is the fourth leading risk factor for premature death annually, according to a 2023 study published in *[JAMA](541941.md)*.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304] Over the last three decades the poor in America have been [incarcerated](1021698.md) at a much higher rate than their counterparts in other developed nations, with penal confinement being "commonplace for poor men of working age".[^cite_note-305] Some scholars contend that the shift to [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberalism) social and economic policies starting in the late 1970s has expanded the penal state, retrenched the social [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state), deregulated the economy and criminalized poverty, ultimately "transforming what it means to be poor in America".[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308]

Sociologist [Matthew Desmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Desmond) writes in his 2023 book *[Poverty, by America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty,_by_America)* that the US "offers some of the lowest wages in the industrialized world", which has "swelled the ranks of the working poor, most of whom are thirty-five or older".[^cite_note-309] Social scientist [Mark Robert Rank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Robert_Rank) asserts that the high rates of poverty in the U.S. can largely be explained as structural failures at the economic and political levels.[^cite_note-310]

[^cite_note-313]

Main article: [Health care in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_the_United_States)
