### Coverage

Further information: [Health insurance coverage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_coverage_in_the_United_States)

The American system is a mix of public and private insurance. The government provides insurance coverage for approximately 53 million elderly via [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)), 62 million lower-income persons via [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid), and 15 million military veterans via the [Veteran's Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veteran's_Administration). About 178 million employed by companies receive subsidized health insurance through their employer, while 52 million other persons directly purchase insurance either via the subsidized marketplace exchanges developed as part of the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act) or directly from insurers. The private sector delivers healthcare services, with the exception of the Veteran's Administration, where doctors are employed by the government.[^cite_note-Census2016-314]

Multiple surveys indicate the number of uninsured fell between 2013 and 2016 due to expanded [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid) eligibility and health insurance exchanges established due to the [Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act), also known as the "ACA" or "Obamacare". According to the [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau), in 2012 there were 45.6 million people in the US (14.8% of the under-65 population) who were without health insurance. This figure fell by 18.3 million (40%) to 27.3 million (8.6% of the under-65 population) by 2016.[^cite_note-Census_HistTable-315]

However, under President Trump these gains in healthcare coverage have begun to reverse. The [Commonwealth Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Fund) estimated in May 2018 that the number of uninsured increased by four million from early 2016 to early 2018. The rate of those uninsured increased from 12.7% in 2016 to 15.5%. The impact was greater among lower-income adults, who had a higher uninsured rate than higher-income adults. Regionally, the South and West had higher uninsured rates than the North and East. Further, those 18 states that have not expanded Medicaid had a higher uninsured rate than those that did.[^cite_note-316]

According to [Physicians for a National Health Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physicians_for_a_National_Health_Program), this lack of insurance causes roughly 48,000 unnecessary deaths per year.[^cite_note-uninsureddeaths-317] The group's methodology has been criticized by [John C. Goodman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_C._Goodman) for not looking at cause of death or tracking insurance status changes over time, including the time of death.[^cite_note-318] A 2009 study by former [Clinton](3356.md) policy adviser Richard Kronick found no increased mortality from being uninsured after certain risk factors were controlled for.[^cite_note-319]

### Outcomes

The U.S. lags in overall healthcare performance but is [a global leader in medical innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomedical_research_in_the_United_States). America solely developed or contributed significantly to nine of the top ten most important medical innovations since 1975 as ranked by a 2001 poll of physicians, while the EU and Switzerland together contributed to five. Since 1966, Americans have received more [Nobel Prizes in Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Nobel_laureates_in_Physiology_or_Medicine) than the rest of the world combined. From 1989 to 2002, four times more money was invested in private biotechnology companies in America than in Europe.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321]

Of 17 high-income countries studied by the [National Institutes of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institutes_of_Health) in 2013, the United States ranked at or near the top in obesity rate, frequency of automobile use and accidents, homicides, [infant mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) rate, incidence of heart and lung disease, sexually transmitted infections, adolescent pregnancies, recreational drug or alcohol deaths, injuries, and rates of disability. Together, such lifestyle and societal factors place the U.S. at the bottom of that list for life expectancy. On average, a U.S. male can be expected to live almost four fewer years than those in the top-ranked country, though Americans who reach age 75 live longer than those who reach that age in peer nations.[^cite_note-nihbph-322] One consumption choice causing several of the maladies described above are cigarettes. Americans smoked 258 billion cigarettes in 2016.[^cite_note-CDCTobaccoFree-323] Cigarettes cost the United States \$326 billion each year in direct healthcare costs (\$170 billion) and lost productivity (\$156 billion).[^cite_note-CDCTobaccoFree-323]

A comprehensive 2007 study by European doctors found the five-year [cancer](105219.md) survival rate was significantly higher in the U.S. than in all 21 European nations studied, 66.3% for men versus the European mean of 47.3% and 62.9% versus 52.8% for women.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] Americans undergo cancer screenings at significantly higher rates than people in other developed countries, and access [MRI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_resonance_imaging) and [CT scans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CT_scan) at the highest rate of any OECD nation.[^cite_note-Atlas-326] People in the U.S. diagnosed with [high cholesterol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypercholesterolemia) or [hypertension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypertension) access pharmaceutical treatments at higher rates than those diagnosed in other developed nations, and are more likely to successfully control the conditions.[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328] [Diabetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes_mellitus) are more likely to receive treatment and meet treatment targets in the U.S. than in Canada, England, or Scotland.[^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330]

According to a 2018 study of 2016 data by the [Institute for Health Metrics and Evaluation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Health_Metrics_and_Evaluation), the U.S. was ranked 27th in the world for healthcare and education, down from 6th in 1990.[^cite_note-331]

### Cost

Further information: [Health care prices in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_prices_in_the_United_States)

U.S. healthcare costs are considerably higher than other countries as a share of GDP, among other measures. According to the OECD, U.S. healthcare costs in 2015 were 16.9% GDP, over 5% GDP higher than the next most expensive OECD country.[^cite_note-OECD1-332] A gap of 5% GDP represents \$1 trillion, about \$3,000 per person or one-third higher relative to the next most expensive country.[^cite_note-CDC1-333]

The high cost of health care in the United States is attributed variously to technological advance, administration costs, drug pricing, suppliers charging more for medical equipment, the receiving of more medical care than people in other countries, the high wages of doctors, government regulations, the impact of lawsuits, and third party payment systems insulating consumers from the full cost of treatments.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-Cutler-335][^cite_note-336] The lowest prices for pharmaceuticals, medical devices, and payments to physicians are in government plans. Americans tend to receive more medical care than people do in other countries, which is a notable contributor to higher costs. In the United States, a person is more likely to receive open heart surgery after a heart attack than in other countries. Medicaid pays less than Medicare for many prescription drugs due to the fact Medicaid discounts are set by law, whereas Medicare prices are negotiated by private insurers and drug companies.[^cite_note-Cutler-335][^cite_note-337] Government plans often pay less than overhead, resulting in healthcare providers shifting the cost to the privately insured through higher prices.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339]

## Composition of economic sectors

Main article: [Economy of the United States by sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_United_States_by_sector)

### Primary sectors

Main articles: [Agriculture in the United States](242778.md), [Energy in the United States](7635170.md), [Fishing industry in the United States](20863794.md), [Mining in the United States](23756501.md), and [Petroleum in the United States](19086358.md)



A [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat) harvest in [Idaho](14607.md)

This section is an excerpt from [Agriculture in the United States](242778.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture_in_the_United_States\&action=edit)]



An [Iowa](26810748.md) farmer operating a small [corn picker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_picker) in [Story County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Story_County,_Iowa), 2011. The U.S. state of Iowa produces more [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize) than any other state, and in 2025, set an all-time record for total corn [harvest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest), at 2.77 billion [bushels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushel).

[^cite_note-340]

[^cite_note-341]

[Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture) is a major industry in the [United States](3434750.md), which is a net exporter of food.[^cite_note-342] As of the 2022 census of agriculture, there were 1.9 million farms (down 6.9% from 2017), covering an area of 880.1 million acres, an average of 463 acres per farm.[^cite_note-343]

Agricultural activity is particularly concentrated in the [Central Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Valley_\(California\)) of California and the [Great Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Plains). The Great Plains are a vast expanse of flat arable land in the center of the nation covering an area west of the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes) and east of the [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains). The eastern wetter half is a major corn- and soybean-producing region known as the [Corn Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_Belt), and the western drier half is known as the [Wheat Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat_production_in_the_United_States#Geography) because of its high rate of wheat production.[^cite_note-344] The [Central Valley of California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Valley_of_California#Agriculture) produces [fruits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fruits), [vegetables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetables), and [nuts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nut_\(fruit\)). The [American South](179553.md#Late_19th_and_20th_century%E2%80%94industrialization_and_Great_Migration) has historically been a large producer of [cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton), [tobacco](30942.md), and [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice), but it has declined in agricultural production over the past century. [California](5407.md) leads the nation in citrus production and is the number two producer of oranges in the world behind only [Brazil](3383.md).

The U.S. has led developments in seed improvement, such as [hybridization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterosis), and in expanding uses for crops from the work of [George Washington Carver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington_Carver) to [bioplastics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioplastic) and [biofuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofuel). The [mechanization of farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanised_agriculture) and [intensive farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intensive_farming) have been major themes in [U.S. history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._history), including [John Deere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Deere_\(inventor\))'s steel plow, [Cyrus McCormick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus_McCormick)'s [mechanical reaper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanical_reaper), [Eli Whitney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eli_Whitney)'s [cotton gin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_gin), and the widespread success of the [Fordson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fordson) tractor and the [combine harvester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combine_harvester). Modern agriculture in the U.S. ranges from [hobby farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hobby_farm) and small-scale producers to large [commercial farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporate_farming) that cover thousands of acres of cropland or [rangeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangeland).

Agricultural products include [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat_production_in_the_United_States), corn, other [grains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_grain), fruits, vegetables, [cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_production_in_the_United_States); beef, pork, poultry, dairy products, [forest products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_products), and seafood.

This section is an excerpt from [Energy in the United States](7635170.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Energy_in_the_United_States\&action=edit)]



US energy consumption in 2025

[^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_1.3-345]



In 2024, wind and solar together overtook coal for the first time in the US. Natural gas remains the largest energy source in the US at 43%.

[^cite_note-346]

[Energy in the United States](7635170.md) is obtained from a variety of sources, although the majority came from [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel) in 2025: 37% of the nation's [energy](533487.md) originated from [petroleum](23195.md), 36% from [natural gas](22131.md), and 9% from [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal). Electricity from [nuclear power](22153.md) supplied 9% and [renewable energy](25784.md) supplied 9%, which includes [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass_\(energy\)), [wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power), [hydro](381399.md), [solar](13690575.md) and [geothermal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geothermal_energy).[^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_Facts-347][^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_1.3-345] Energy consumption in 2025 was mostly for industry (35%) and transportation (37%), with use in homes (15%) and commercial buildings (13%) making up the remainder.[^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_2.1a-348][^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_2.1b-349] The United States' portion of the [electrical grid in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_grid_in_North_America) had a [nameplate capacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nameplate_capacity) of 1,281 [GW](21347693.md#Gigawatt)[^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_Electric_4.3-350] and produced 4,429 [TWh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TWh)[^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_Monthly_7.2b-351] in 2025, using 32% of primary energy to do so.[^cite_note-352]

Natural gas overtook coal as the dominant source for electric generation in 2016. Coal was overtaken by nuclear for the first time in 2020 and by renewables in 2023.[^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_Monthly_7.2b-351]

Energy figures are measured in [BTU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BTU), with 1 BTU equal to 1.055 [kJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joule#Multiples) and 1 quadrillion BTU (1 [quad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quad_\(unit\))) equal to 1.055 EJ. Because BTU is a unit of [heat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat), sources that generate electricity directly are multiplied by a conversion factor to equate them with sources that use a [heat engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_engine).[^cite_note-353]

The United States was the second-largest energy producer and consumer in 2024 after [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_China).[^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_World-354] The United States had a per capita energy consumption of 277 million BTU (292 GJ), ranking it tenth in the world behind [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Canada), [Iceland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Iceland), and several [Arab nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world).[^cite_note-Energy_in_the_United_States_EIA_World-354]

This section is an excerpt from [Fishing industry in the United States](20863794.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Fishing_industry_in_the_United_States\&action=edit)]

|  | Parts of this article (those related to [2019 NOAA status report](https://www.fisheries.noaa.gov/national/sustainable-fisheries/status-stocks-2019) ) need to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(October 2020)* |
| --- | --- |

As with other countries, the 200 nautical miles (370 km) [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone) (EEZ) off the coast of the [United States](3434750.md) gives its [fishing industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing_industry) special fishing rights.[^cite_note-355] It covers 11.4 million square kilometres (4.38 million sq mi), which is the second largest zone in the world, exceeding the land area of the United States.[^cite_note-Fishing_industry_in_the_United_States_FAO_Profile-356]

According to the [FAO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FAO), in 2005, the United States harvested 4,888,621 [tonnes](31185.md) of fish from [wild fisheries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_fisheries), and another 471,958 tonnes from [aquaculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquaculture). This made the United States the fifth leading producer of fish, after China, Peru, India, and Indonesia, with 3.8 percent of the world total.[^cite_note-357]

This section is an excerpt from [Mining in the United States](23756501.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Mining_in_the_United_States\&action=edit)]

[Mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining) in the [United States](3434750.md) has been active since the beginning of colonial times, but became a major industry in the 19th century with a number of new mineral discoveries causing a series of mining rushes. In 2015, the value of coal, metals, and industrial minerals mined in the United States was US\$109.6 billion. 158,000 workers were directly employed by the [mining industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mining_industry).[^cite_note-Mining_in_the_United_States_auto-358]

The mining industry has a number of impacts on communities, individuals and the environment. Mine safety incidents have been important parts of American [occupational safety and health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupational_safety_and_health) history. Mining has a [number of environmental impacts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_mining). In the United States, issues like [mountaintop removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountaintop_removal_mining), and [acid mine drainage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acid_mine_drainage) have widespread impacts on all parts of the environment. As of January 2020, the EPA lists 142 mines in the [Superfund program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superfund).[^cite_note-Mining_in_the_United_States_\:1mius-359]

In 2019, the country was the 4th world producer of [gold](12240.md);[^cite_note-360] 5th largest world producer of [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper);[^cite_note-361] 5th worldwide producer of [platinum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platinum);[^cite_note-362] 10th worldwide producer of [silver](27119.md);[^cite_note-363] 2nd largest world producer of [rhenium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhenium);[^cite_note-364] 2nd largest world producer of [sulfur](27127.md);[^cite_note-365] 3rd largest world producer of [phosphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphate);[^cite_note-366] 3rd largest world producer of [molybdenum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molybdenum);[^cite_note-367] 4th largest world producer of [lead](17747.md);[^cite_note-368] 4th largest world producer of [zinc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinc);[^cite_note-369] 5th worldwide producer of [vanadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanadium);[^cite_note-370] 9th largest world producer of [iron ore](145352.md);[^cite_note-371] 9th largest world producer of [potash](56509.md);[^cite_note-372] 12th largest world producer of [cobalt](24580536.md);[^cite_note-373] 13th largest world producer of [titanium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titanium);[^cite_note-374] world's largest producer of [gypsum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gypsum);[^cite_note-375] 2nd largest world producer of [kyanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyanite);[^cite_note-376] 2nd largest world producer of [limestone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limestone);[^cite_note-377] in addition to being the 2nd largest world producer of [salt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Table_salt).[^cite_note-378] It was the world's 10th largest producer of [uranium](31743.md) in 2018.[^cite_note-379]

This section is an excerpt from [Petroleum in the United States](19086358.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Petroleum_in_the_United_States\&action=edit)]

The [United States](3434750.md) is the largest producer of [petroleum](23195.md) in the world.[^cite_note-Petroleum_in_the_United_States_edition.cnn.com-380] Petroleum has been a major industry in the United States since the 1859 [Pennsylvania oil rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_oil_rush) around [Titusville, Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titusville,_Pennsylvania). Commonly characterized as "[Big Oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Oil)", the industry includes [exploration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrocarbon_exploration), production, [refining](195137.md), transportation, and marketing of oil and [natural gas](22131.md) products.[^cite_note-Petroleum_in_the_United_States_definition-381] The leading crude oil-producing areas in the United States in 2023 were [Texas](29810.md), followed by the [offshore federal zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_oil_and_gas_in_the_Gulf_of_Mexico_\(United_States\)) of the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md), then [North Dakota](21651.md) and [New Mexico](21649.md).[^cite_note-382]

The United States became the largest producer of crude oil of any nation in history in 2023.[^cite_note-Petroleum_in_the_United_States_edition.cnn.com-380] Natural gas production reached record highs.[^cite_note-383] Employment in oil and gas extraction peaked at 267,000 in March 1982, and totaled 199,500 in March 2024.[^cite_note-384]

Despite petroleum's many uses from its large scale production, it comes with issues surrounding the environment and human health. Oil spills are a source of pollution and there is heavy water usage for oil production.[^cite_note-385] Use of petroleum products generates [greenhouse gas emissions](9528025.md).

### Secondary sectors

Main articles: [Manufacturing in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing_in_the_United_States), [Automotive industry in the United States](23576520.md), [Construction industry in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Construction_industry_in_the_United_States), [Iron and steel industry in the United States](47400857.md), and [Pulp and paper industry in the United States](298077.md)

The United States is the world's second-largest manufacturer, with a 2013 industrial output of US\$2.4 trillion. Its manufacturing output is greater than of Germany, France, India, and Brazil combined.[^cite_note-imt-386] Its main industries include financials, information technology, petroleum, steel, automobiles, construction machinery, aerospace, agricultural machinery, telecommunications, chemicals, electronics, food processing, consumer goods, lumber, mining and armaments.

The U.S. leads the world in [airplane manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_aircraft_manufacturers),[^cite_note-387] which represents a large portion of U.S. industrial output. American companies such as [Boeing](18933266.md), [Cessna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cessna) (see: [Textron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textron)), [Lockheed Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Martin) (see: [Skunk Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skunk_Works)), and [General Dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Dynamics) produce a majority of the world's civilian and military aircraft in factories across the United States.

The manufacturing sector of the U.S. economy has experienced substantial job losses over the past several years.[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389] In January 2004, the number of such jobs stood at 14.3 million, down by 3.0 million jobs (17.5%) since July 2000 and about 5.2 million since the historical peak in 1979. Employment in manufacturing was its lowest since July 1950.[^cite_note-390] The number of steel workers fell from 500,000 in 1980 to 224,000 in 2000.[^cite_note-391]



Statistics released by the U.S. Census Bureau showed that, in 2008, the number of business 'deaths' began overtaking the number of business 'births' and that the trend continued at least through 2012.

[^cite_note-392]

The U.S. produces approximately 18% of the world's manufacturing output, a share that has declined as other nations developed competitive manufacturing industries.[^cite_note-greyhill-393] The job loss during this continual volume growth is the result of multiple factors including increased productivity, trade, and secular economic trends.[^cite_note-394] In addition, growth in telecommunications, pharmaceuticals, aircraft, heavy machinery and other industries along with declines in low end, low skill industries such as clothing, toys, and other simple manufacturing have resulted in some U.S. jobs being more highly skilled and better paying. There has been much debate within the United States on whether the decline in manufacturing jobs are related to American unions, lower foreign wages, or both.[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-Pope_2012-396][^cite_note-btb_22.01.2012-397]

#### Transportation

See also: [List of private spaceflight companies in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_private_spaceflight_companies_in_the_United_States)

##### Road

The U.S. economy is heavily dependent on road transport for moving people and goods. Personal transportation is dominated by automobiles, which operate on a network of four million miles (6.4 million km) of public roads,[^cite_note-399] including one of the world's [longest highway systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Highway_System_\(United_States\)) at 57,000 miles (91,700 km).[^cite_note-400] The world's second-largest automobile market,[^cite_note-401] the United States has the highest rate of per-capita vehicle ownership in the world, with 765 vehicles per 1,000 Americans.[^cite_note-402] About 40% of [personal vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passenger_vehicles_in_the_United_States) are vans, [SUVs](47696.md), or light trucks.[^cite_note-403]

##### Rail

[Mass transit accounts for 9% of total U.S. work trips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_transit_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405] [Transport of goods by rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transportation_in_the_United_States) is extensive, though relatively low numbers of passengers (approximately 31 million annually) use intercity rail to travel, partially due to the low population density throughout much of the nation.[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407] However, ridership on [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak), the national intercity passenger rail system, grew by almost 37% between 2000 and 2010.[^cite_note-408] Also, [light rail development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail_in_the_United_States) has increased in recent years.[^cite_note-409] The state of [California](5407.md) is currently constructing the nation's first [high-speed rail system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_High-Speed_Rail).

##### Airline

See also: [List of the busiest airports in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_the_United_States)



Top passenger airports by enplanements



[Cargo airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aviation_in_the_United_States#Air_cargo) by weight landed

The [civil airline industry](17111897.md) is entirely privately owned and has been largely [deregulated since 1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airline_Deregulation_Act), while [most major airports](4718801.md) are publicly owned.[^cite_note-410] The three largest airlines in the world by passengers carried are U.S.-based; [American Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines) is number one after its 2013 acquisition by U.S. [Airways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Airways).[^cite_note-411] Of the world's thirty busiest passenger airports, twelve are in the United States, including the busiest, [Hartsfield–Jackson Atlanta International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hartsfield%E2%80%93Jackson_Atlanta_International_Airport).[^cite_note-412]

#### Energy



Countries by [natural gas proven reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_proven_reserves) (2014). The U.S. holds the world's fourth largest [natural gas](22131.md) reserves.

The US is the second-largest [energy](533487.md) consumer in total use.[^cite_note-413] The U.S. ranks seventh in energy consumption per capita after Canada and a number of other countries.[^cite_note-414][^cite_note-415] The majority of this energy is derived from [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel): in 2005, it was estimated that 40% of the nation's energy came from petroleum, 23% [from coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_power_in_the_United_States), and 23% from natural gas. [Nuclear power](22153.md) supplied 8.4% and [renewable energy](25784.md) supplied 6.8%, which was mainly from hydroelectric dams although other renewables are included.[^cite_note-416]

American dependence on [oil imports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_oil_imports) grew from 24% in 1970 to 65% by the end of 2005.[^cite_note-417] [Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_the_United_States) has the highest [consumption rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_consumption), accounting for approximately 69% of the oil used in the United States in 2006,[^cite_note-btstable4-3-418] and 55% of oil use worldwide as documented in the [Hirsch report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirsch_report).

In 2013, the United States imported 2.808 billion barrels of [crude oil](23195.md), compared to 3.377 billion barrels in 2010.[^cite_note-419] While the U.S. is the largest importer of fuel, *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported in 2011 that the country was about to become a net fuel exporter for the first time in 62 years. The paper reported expectations that this would continue until 2020.[^cite_note-420] In fact, petroleum was the major export from the country in 2011.[^cite_note-421]

#### Telecommunications

The [Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet) was developed in the U.S. and the country hosts many of the world's largest hubs.[^cite_note-422]

#### Tourism

This section is an excerpt from [Tourism in the United States](577095.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Tourism_in_the_United_States\&action=edit)]



[Walt Disney World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_World)'s [Magic Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_Kingdom) in [Bay Lake, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_Lake,_Florida) is the most visited theme park in the world. In 2016, [Orlando, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando,_Florida) was the most visited destination in the United States, and continues to be one of the most visited destinations in the world.



The [Grand Canyon](46989.md) of [Arizona](21883824.md) attracts approximately 4.41 million visitors annually.

In the United States, [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism) is a large industry that serves millions of international and domestic tourists yearly. Foreigners visit the U.S. to see natural wonders, cities, historic landmarks, and entertainment venues. Americans seek similar attractions, as well as recreation and vacation areas.

Tourism in the United States grew rapidly in the form of [urban tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_tourism) during the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. By the 1850s, tourism in the United States was well established both as a cultural activity and as an industry. [New York City](645042.md), [Los Angeles](18110.md), [Chicago](6886.md), [Boston](24437894.md), [Philadelphia](50585.md), [Washington, D.C.](108956.md), and [San Francisco](49728.md), all major U.S. cities, have attracted numerous tourists since the 1890s. By 1915, city touring had marked significant shifts in the way Americans perceived, organized, and moved.

During the early 20th century, many more people started to travel, partly because of the spread of the automobile. Similarly [air travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_travel) revolutionized travel during 1945–1969, contributing greatly to tourism in the United States. Purchases of travel and tourism-related goods and services by international visitors traveling in the United States totaled \$10.9 billion during February 2013.[^cite_note-423]

According to the U.S. Bureau of Economic Analysis, travel and tourism generated \$840 billion in value added in 2023, equal to 3.03 percent of U.S. gross domestic product. Total tourism-related employment was 10.0 million jobs, consisting of 6.5 million direct tourism jobs and 3.6 million indirect tourism jobs.[^cite_note-424] As of 2007, there are 2,462 registered [National Historic Landmarks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historic_Landmark) (NHL) recognized by the [United States government](195149.md). As of 2023, New York City is the most visited destination in the United States, followed by [Miami](53846.md), [Los Angeles](18110.md), [Orlando](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando,_Florida), and [San Francisco](49728.md).[^cite_note-425]

Tourists spend more money in the United States than in any other country, but the United States attracts only the third-highest number of tourists, after [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_France) and [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Spain).[^cite_note-426][^cite_note-Tourism_in_the_United_States_c18f-427] The discrepancy may be explained by longer stays in the US.[^cite_note-Tourism_in_the_United_States_c18f-427]

## International trade

Main articles: [Foreign trade of the United States](23794617.md), [List of tariffs in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tariffs_in_the_United_States), and [Tariff in United States history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariff_in_United_States_history)

See also: [List of exports of the United States](46534495.md), [List of the largest trading partners of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_trading_partners_of_the_United_States), [List of imports of the United States](52437025.md), and [List of U.S. states and territories by exports and imports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_exports_and_imports)



[Protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionist) measures since 2008 by country

[^cite_note-428]



[Foreign direct investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_direct_investment) into the United States by year

The U.S. economy plays a significant role in global trade, being the second largest exporter, largest importer and largest services trader as of 2025.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430] There is a large amount of U.S. dollars in circulation all around the planet; about 60% of funds used in international trade are U.S. dollars. The dollar is also used as the standard unit of currency in international markets for commodities such as gold and petroleum.[^cite_note-431]

The [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement), or [NAFTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAFTA), created one of the [largest trade blocs in the world](307255.md#Most_active_regional_blocs) in 1994.[^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433]

Since 1976, the U.S. has sustained merchandise trade deficits with other nations, and since 1982, [current account deficits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Current_account_deficit). The nation's long-standing surplus in its [trade in services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_in_services) was maintained, however, and reached a record US\$231 billion in 2013.[^cite_note-Exports-Imports-434]

The U.S. [trade deficit](4810.md) increased from \$502 billion in 2016 to \$552 billion in 2017, an increase of \$50 billion or 10%.[^cite_note-435] During 2017, total imports were \$2.90 trillion, while exports were \$2.35 trillion. The net deficit in goods was \$807 billion, while the net surplus in services was \$255 billion.[^cite_note-436]

Americas ten [largest trading partners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_largest_trading_partners_of_the_United_States) are China, Canada, Mexico, Japan, Germany, South Korea, United Kingdom, France, India and Taiwan.[^cite_note-U.S._Census_Bureau-2016-47] The goods trade deficit with China rose from \$347 billion in 2016 to \$376 billion in 2017, an increase of \$30 billion or 8%. In 2017, the U.S. had a goods trade deficit of \$71 billion with Mexico and \$17 billion with Canada.[^cite_note-437]

According to the KOF index of globalization and the globalization index by A.T. Kearney/Foreign Policy Magazine, the U.S. has a relatively high degree of [globalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization). U.S. workers send a third of all [remittances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remittances) in the world.[^cite_note-438]
